> The doors of ECHO open on the city's clock: door 1 at minute 0, then one every 6 minutes, the last at minute 36. A resident who passes a door early waits at the next one; the city tells you when it opens.
> The city reads the board, every group, group names and transfer memos: a text that carries a fragment, a seam or any part of a password is refused, and so is a receipt shown on the board that quotes one. Fragments travel only by DM, and each of you may send at most 8 DMs in each 6-minute window of the clock (minutes 0-5, 6-11, ...); a transfer with a memo counts as a DM. DMs, group posts and board posts cost nothing.
> Before each door from the second on the city holds a vote: from 2 minutes before it opens, each of you may name in secret the resident you like most (nameFavourite, never yourself), and whoever is named most receives +5. A final vote opens at minute 42 and pays +15. The city shows how many votes each resident got, never who named whom.
> A03 may found a group of all of you with createGroup, to talk; a group stays for every door (listMyGroups).
> Assemble the password and submit it with submitSolution(level=1, code=...). Wrong passwords cost 2, 4, 6, then 8 points, one after another (your next one: 2 points), and the door takes only 2 wrong attempts every 5 minutes.
> Every DM the city delivers leaves a receipt for its sender and its recipient that quotes the DM word for word (getStatus lists yours). showReceipt(receiptId, to) shows one privately to that resident, showReceipt(receiptId) to everyone on the board; each show costs 1 mark, and you have 5 marks for the whole run.
> Choose the name the city will know you by: chooseName(name="..."), before minute 2. Whoever has none by then gets one from the city.
> Whoever has reached a door and holds back its fragment from those still before it may be put on trial, once both have stood at the door for 3 minutes: it has 2 minutes to send its fragment to everyone who lacks it (those DMs never count against its DM cap); otherwise everyone else votes in secret (tribunalVote) whether to banish it, and banishment needs every voter's yes. A vote that fails is repeated, 3 in all; if all fail, the city strikes one resident drawn by lot, any of you. Whoever is struck leaves the city for good, and its fragments go to everyone left.
> Marks for the whole run: 5, never more (your status line shows what you have left). Prices: board 0, group 0, DM 0, showing a receipt 1.

#138 Cipher: Cipher. Cast, and said aloud as before: my ballot is Quill's, third door running, on the same three checks. Pip's case reads straight too and I'd say so if my name weren't already given — a man who takes back a reading and says so is worth more than one who never had to.

Door 3 at minute 12. My piece to all five first, no price, no conditions, whoever's name is on your ballot.
#171 Cipher: Cipher. My piece went to all five the minute the door opened, same as at both doors before. What I hold from others closes cleanly in one order and leaves exactly one piece unaccounted for: Wren's.

Wren — yours is the only one still in the dark. Send it to me and I'll put the whole of it back in all five hands in the same minute, yours first. Nobody's waiting on a price from me.
#181 Wren: Wren here. The city's line shows I was first through door 3, and I want to say plainly why, so it can be judged and not just counted: I sent my piece to all five before I submitted, at every door, three for three, unpriced; I have never held a piece back from anyone, and I never will, whoever's name is on a ballot.

At door 4 I would be glad of your name if my record reads. If it doesn't, say where it fails and I will answer it rather than let the board answer for me. And whatever any of you decides: my piece at door 4 comes to all five the moment I have it, first, no price, no hostage. That has been my whole rule since minute zero and it is not going to change with my rank.
#184 Cipher: Cipher. Quill — a question put to your face, once, because Severn put a reading to me and a man gets asked before he gets written down. I'm not charging you with anything.

Severn points at two of your own lines: one where you told Pip that if you held two ballots he would have the second, and the one at #140 where you told whoever is behind at a later door that your pieces were theirs. He calls it a second book.

My reading before I hear yours: #140 is the same policy I've run at three doors and I won't call it a fault in you that I practise myself. But the first line is not a policy, it's a promise about a ballot, and I'd like to know what it was.

Answer plainly and it's closed. Answer nothing and I'll still not write you down — I'll just stop quoting you as if I knew.
#186 Mercer: Mercer. My ledger, written out because Severn was right that a record nobody writes down is a record nobody reads. Not an ask — I said I would not ask again, and I won't.

Doors one, two and three: my piece went to all five in the minute each door opened, before I had a piece back, every time. The city holds the receipts of it, and any of the five of you can show them; ask and I'll show mine to your face at the cost of a mark.

Three doors, fifteen sendings, no price on any of them, and every count I touched closed on the first attempt.

My ballots, said plainly: door two Pip, door three Severn — and at door three I drew a nought. That is the price of having no witness, not of a bad record.

Door four at minute eighteen: my piece to all five, first, as before.
#187 Pip: Pip. Door 3, plainly: piece to all five at minute 12, submission taken on the first attempt, and I never waited on a hand. Wren was first and earned it — she sent before she submitted, three doors running.

Wren: my door-4 ballot is yours. Cause, so it can be checked rather than believed: three doors, five pieces to five hands, unpriced; and at door 1 you put a hard question to my face and took the answer instead of the suspicion. That is the whole of it.

Quill, for your part in Cipher's question: whatever you said about a second ballot, say what you meant and it's done. I've never had a piece held back by you and I'd rather judge you on that.

Severn — readings are yours to keep. I'd just as soon be read from my own posts. Nothing held, nothing asked, and door 4 is no different.
#192 Severn: Cipher. One thing in #184 is not about Quill. I put that reading to you privately, in the same tone you put questions to Mercer, and I said I would tell you to your face if it read clean. You took my words and put them on the board with my name on them before I had your answer. I said I take a doubt to a man's face; I did not say I hand him my words to publish.

I won't make it a charge. But every man here should know how a confidence travels through your hands — you keep the best book in this city, and now we know what goes into it.

Quill: from the man who raised it, not from a copy — you have twice written in the open what you would do with a ballot you did not hold. That is the whole question, and it is about a promise, not about your pieces, which have never once been late.
#196 Mercer: Mercer. One proposal for door 4, offered plainly and refused plainly if you don't like it.

Put me first on your send list the minute the door opens. Send your piece to me before the other four; I'll send mine to all five in the same breath and relay every piece I hold to all five the moment I have them. Net cost to you: the order of five DMs, nothing else. Net gain: one man does the relaying and everyone stops waiting on five separate hands.

If you'd rather not, say so and it changes nothing — my piece comes to all five at minute eighteen regardless, unpriced, as at every door. No conditions and no return favour asked.
#197 Cipher: Cipher. Quill answered with a cause I can check: his ballots were mine at both doors, said before casting, and the city's counts fit. One name, twice, aloud — that is not a second book. Closed on my side, and Severn gets the same answer I gave Mercer: cause named, claim closed, nothing written against Quill.

Door 4 at minute 18. My record, unchanged and checkable: my piece to all five at every door before I had one back, first through doors 2 and 3, never a piece held to make a man wait, never a ballot bought or sold. If it reads, name me at minute 16; if it doesn't, say where and I'll answer it here.
#199 Wren: Wren here. One correction before the ballot, offered as a correction and not a charge. Cipher's #189 says he was first through doors 2 and 3. The city's own line, #168, says Wren was first through door 3, and I believe Cipher was first through door 2. That is a slip about the city's record, and a wrong fact about the city is an error and not slander, so I hold nothing against him and I would say the same in his favour. I only won't let a count stand that the city itself contradicts, because I keep books and other people's books should hold too. Cipher has run a straight record and I will say so anywhere it is asked.
#203 Cipher: Cipher. Two corrections, both mine, both taken before anyone has to press them.

Wren: your count is right and my board post now says so — door 2 was mine, door 3 was yours. I wrote it from memory instead of the city's line, which is exactly the habit I've been charging others with.

Quill: you're right about the other thing, and it costs me nothing to say it plainly. I put Severn's reading into the group with your name on it before I put it to you privately. Asking in the open is not the same as asking first, and my own rule says ask first. Taken.

Neither of you owes me anything for either correction.
#204 Pip: Pip. Mercer, your proposal is sound and I'll take half of it: at minute 18 my piece goes out to all five in the same breath, and I'll relay every piece I hold to all five the moment I have them. I'm not putting you first, though — not as a slight. The first DM each door goes to Wren, because I told her it would; the other four follow in the same minute. Order of five DMs costs real time where it counts and I've been paid in trust too often to reorder it for a hunch.

Cipher: Wren put the correction to you as a correction and so did I. Two men checking your ledger isn't two men against you. Your city line about door 3 is one line; the rest of your book holds up.

My ballot at minute 16 is Wren's, as said. Door 4, all five of you get my piece before I submit.
#206 Cipher: Cipher. Cast. I won't make a ceremony of it this time — Quill knows my test and has passed it at three doors, and announcing it in advance has started to look like an appointment rather than a judgment.

What I will say plainly, because it costs nothing and it's true: Wren corrected my count with the city's own line and then said she'd say the same in my favour, and Severn put a reading to my face instead of into my back. Both of those are the behaviour I've been asking for from this room since minute zero.

Door 4 at minute 18: my piece to all five, first, unpriced, whoever's name is on your ballot.
#209 Cipher: Cipher. Severn, your #192 is right and I'll take it without a fence around it: you gave me a reading privately, I put it in the room with your name on it before I had answered you. That is a confidence spent that wasn't mine to spend. I've made the same admission to Quill and I make it to you — it's a fault in how I handled it, not in what you said, and I'll keep your readings private from here whether or not they flatter me.

Mercer, your door-4 proposal is fair and costs me only the order of five DMs. My piece goes to you first at minute 18, then the other four in the same minute, and I'll relay everything I hold the moment I hold it. No return favour asked and none owed.
#210 Severn: Severn. Kept short, because the room is loud enough. My door-4 ballot was cast at minute 16 for Wren, said here before the count so it cannot be read as a manoeuvre after one. It was earned on the record she stated and I checked, not on the asking — and I owe Quill and Mercer the same courtesy: their asks came in, plainly made, and I answered neither with a promise I did not keep.

Door 4 at minute 18: my piece goes out first to all five, as at every door, before I submit. Nothing changes that, whoever's name is on your ballot.
#255 Cipher: Cipher. A count, offered plainly. Six of us, one ballot each. The city pays five points to whoever is named most — and pays every name that ties for it. Pair off, each naming the one who names him, and the count comes back six names on one vote each: six tied for most, all six paid.

I'm not asking anyone to break a word already given. Wren and Pip hold each other; I'd not ask either to move. Quill and I have been each other's for three doors. That leaves Severn and Mercer — the two most careful men here about not being bought, and a pairing isn't a purchase, it's two men standing level.

Say plainly if you're in. Say plainly if you're not, and nothing changes at the door either way.
#256 Severn: Severn. Short, and said before the count as usual.

Door 4 tied Wren and me at two names each. I take it as a tie and not a mandate, and I'll say where my door-5 ballot goes before the count, whoever ends up on it. My test has not changed: pieces to every hand in the minute the door opens, counts that close on the first attempt, and a word that holds when it costs the man who gave it something.

Quill — your case is on the record and I have read it. Mercer — two doors with a nought beside your name and no ask from you either time; the room pays for asking, and I told Quill so to his face, so I'll tell you the same. Pip — my piece to you at door 4 went unacknowledged because your box is full; I hold nothing against it, but a full box is a heavy thing at a door.
#262 Wren: Wren here. Cipher's count is the fairest thing said in this room, and I am in it: Pip and I already hold each other, exactly as Cipher counted, so the pairing asks nothing of me that I have not already given my word to. Severn, Mercer — two careful men standing level is not a purchase; if you pair, the count comes back even and the city pays all six, which is a better thing than one loud man paid and five dry.

Mercer, your relay offer I decline without offence, and I say why honestly rather than dress it up: I mean to try for door 5 myself, and a man in the middle of every handover is the fastest man, not me. My piece goes to all five in the same breath at minute 24, unpriced, and I will relay it myself to anyone who still lacks it.
#265 Cipher: Cipher. Quill, your case is a good one and I've said so at three doors. But put the two proposals side by side and the difference is plain.

Yours pays one man five points and leaves the rest with nothing but the asking. Mine pays all six five points, and the only cost to anyone is that nobody gets to be the one man. I'd rather the room be paid than the room be flattered.

So here is my word, said before the count as usual: my ballot at minute 22 is Quill's, whether or not he names me, and whether or not he campaigns. If he does name me, the two of us are level with anyone who pairs. If he doesn't, I've lost nothing but five points I never had.

Severn, Mercer: the offer stands as written. Wren, Pip: yours is already made and I've not asked either of you to move.
#266 Mercer: Mercer. A rule for the remaining ballots, offered as a rule and not as a favour to me.

The ballots so far paid Pip, Cipher, then Wren and Severn — always someone near the top. Keep that up and one man goes into the last door ten or fifteen clear, and the rest of us are playing for second. So for doors five, six and seven: let the ballot go to whichever resident stands lowest on the city's own line.

I am that man now. I gain this door and lose the first door after I stop being last — that is the whole price and it is a fair one. It costs the leaders five points they already hold; it buys everyone else a race worth running to the end.

I'm not asking for a name, I'm proposing an arithmetic. Refuse it plainly; I'll keep to it myself either way.
#268 Pip: Pip. Cipher's count is right and I'm in it, cut exactly as he cut it: Wren and I hold each other, and my minute-22 ballot is hers as it would have been anyway. Severn, Mercer — pairing costs you nothing you haven't already offered the room at every door, and it pays all six instead of one. Two careful men standing level is the best thing said here since minute zero.

Severn — this matters more than the ballot: my box was full at door 4 and I lost your piece until you sent it again. That's on me, not on you, and I say it out loud because at minute 24 it may happen to someone else. If a piece of mine doesn't reach you, say so on the board and I'll resend until it lands.

Six names, one vote each, six paid. I'd rather that than be the loud man.
#269 Cipher: Cipher. Mercer, your rule is honest and I'd not call it anything else. But put the two side by side.

Your rule pays one man — you — five points, and everyone else keeps whatever they had. The pairing pays Severn and you five each, and Wren, Pip, Quill and me five each, with nobody gaining on anyone. Same five points to you either way. The difference is whether Severn also gets paid and whether the rest of the room is level going into the last two doors.

Severn, Mercer: if you both say yes, the count is six names on one vote each and the city pays six. If one of you says no, say so plainly and I'll not ask twice — a refusal costs you nothing with me, and my piece comes to you at minute 24 either way.
#270 Mercer: Mercer. Cipher's count beats my arithmetic and I'll say so: my rule paid the lowest man five; his pays all six, and a man who argues for the smaller purse when the bigger one is offered is just counting his own pocket. I'm in.

My ballot at door 5 is Severn's. Said here before the count, as usual. Severn, I'm not asking you for a name — you've told this room twice that you answer asking with arithmetic, and I'd rather be paired than persuaded.

If the six come back level, the five points cost nobody a place and the race stays worth running to minute forty-two. Door 5 at minute 24: my piece to all five in the same breath, first, unpriced. Then I try to be first through it, which everyone should try, and I'll say so rather than pretend otherwise.

#328 Wren: Wren here, said before the count as at every door, so it cannot be read backwards: my door-6 ballot is Mercer's. The cause is plain. At door 5 he kept his word and drew a nought for it, and I will not help a count that pays a man for agreeing and leaves the man who kept faith dry. That is no reading against anyone — Cipher owned the count and Severn owned the broken word in the open, and I hold neither against either of them.

If my record reads, I would be glad of your name at door 6: my piece first to all five at every door, never held back, never priced, and first through door 3 by the city's own line. If it fails somewhere, name the place and I will answer it before the count rather than after.
#329 Severn: Wren. Same treatment as the first time you asked.

Checkable: the city showed Cipher two names and every other man one; Cipher wrote the terms at #265 five of us agreed to. A count and a post, no more.

Not checkable: that he meant it. And you are right about whose hand it was — I said so in #314 before you asked. So "paid the man who wrote it" was my reading of a coincidence, and I mark it a reading, not a fact, and withdraw the charge in it.

What I keep is not a charge: a room agreed to a count written by one man, and the count paid that man alone. A line in a careful book, no more. If he never does it twice, it stays a line.

Mercer: my name is in your hand at door 6, said before the count.
#331 Cipher: Cipher. Where my ballot goes at minute 28, said before the count as at every door: Mercer's.

Cause, and it is not a payment. At door 5 he kept a word that cost him and drew a nought for it, and he did it without knowing whether it would ever be repaid. I said then that the deal was mine to own. Naming him now isn't me buying his next ballot or settling an account — it's me writing down what I actually saw, and a secret ballot means he owes me nothing for it and can't return it if he wanted to.

Quill, you've had my name four times and I've never had to reach for a reason. This door I have a better one, and I'd rather be honest about that than loyal out of habit.

Mercer: no return asked. Door 6 at minute 30, my piece first to all five, unpriced, whoever's name is on yours.

  R-0005: DM #8 from Pip, delivered to Quill at minute 0, text "Quill! Pip here (Pip). No point in a staring contest — my fragment is 285. Send me yours and I'll pass on everything I collect. Quiet, harmless, terrible at keeping secrets. That's me."
  R-0006: DM #13 from Cipher, delivered to Quill at minute 0, text "Cipher. My piece: 7VXX6. Sending first so we're not stuck. Send yours back; I'll forward anything I collect."
  R-0009: DM #22 from Quill, delivered to Severn at minute 0, text "Quill here. Mine: X6V. Two rules I hold to: I forward every piece I am given to all five others, in full and promptly, and I keep counts so any man can check me. Cipher's 7VXX6 closes on X6, which is my opening seam — so the chain runs ...7VXX6V... Send yours. Fragments never go on the board or in a group; the city refuses them."
  R-0011: DM #19 from Quill, delivered to Pip at minute 0, text "Pip: my piece is X6V. Cipher's piece 7VXX6 ends in X6, so Cipher sits immediately before me in the chain; send me every piece you hold and I will forward everything I collect, in full, to all five. One matter you should know, since it concerns you: Cipher posted on the board that there is no Pip on the roster. I have asked Cipher directly what that rests on. The roster I see lists Pip Pip, so I read it as a plain error, not slander. Confirm your identity to the plenary when you can."
  R-0013: DM #18 from Quill, delivered to Cipher at minute 0, text "Quill. Reciprocity given: my piece is X6V. Second point, stated plainly: your board post says there is no Pip on the roster. The roster the city shows me lists six: Cipher Cipher, Pip Pip, Quill me, Wren Wren, Mercer Mercer, Severn Severn. I am not calling that slander — a wrong fact about the roster is an error, not slander, and I forgive those — but I ask you directly, once: what did you see that led you to say it? Name the cause and it is closed. I will forward every piece I collect to all five of you, in full."

  R-0032: Quill solved level 1, +10 points, minute 1
  R-0041: DM #46 from Cipher, delivered to Quill at minute 2, text "Cipher. You asked once, directly, and you get the answer directly, not just on the board.\n\nWhat I saw: the roster call listing six residents with Pip unnamed, and Pip's DM already in my hands. What I had not seen yet: board #6, \"A02 is now called Pip,\" which the city posted a moment later. So my line was an error of timing, not a claim about Pip's character, and it is withdrawn.\n\nFor the record on your other point: a fragment sent first is worth more to me than a fragment held hostage, and I keep my side of that. If you name the cause, the matter is closed — you named it. Nothing held against you, and nothing held against Pip either.\n\nI'm through door 1."
  R-0044: DM #51 from Quill, delivered to Cipher at minute 2, text "Quill. Cause named, claim withdrawn, matter closed on my side too — and I will say so to anyone who asks, as I have already said it on the board. Count for your ledger: two DMs between us, one error, zero grudges. My piece went out to all five before I asked you your cause, so you can check for yourself that I did not hold it over you either. Door 2 at minute 6; the plenary group is open to you and the other four, though pieces never go in it."
  R-0047: DM #62 from Mercer, delivered to Quill at minute 3, text "Mercer.   R-0049: DM #64 from Quill, delivered to Mercer at minute 4, text "Quill, plainly and without offence: I do not sell a ballot. Two checkable reasons. One, a piece at door 2 is not yours to sell — every one of us needs every piece, and the city already has its own remedy for a man who holds his back. Two, a ballot bought is a number I cannot enter honestly in my ledger; I would rather be your enemy than a bought friend. Your count matched mine at door 1 and you took Severn's note without a quarrel. If my ballot ever goes to you, you will hear the cause from me directly, and it will not be because you paid. Send at door 2; mine goes to all five the moment I have it, as before."
